It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people or groups you believe are keeping information from you are actually out to harm you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re trying to harm me physically or anything like that. But I do think they’re harming me by keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions about my sister. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, for months now, I keep seeing the same kind of pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget.” Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes or misunderstandings, but it doesn’t feel like that. I feel like information is being kept from me, and like people are trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these patterns you've noticed are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d use the word conspiracy, but it feels like it’s coordinated somehow. It’s the same pattern over and over—my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. After months of that, it doesn’t feel accidental. It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people or groups you believe are keeping information from you are actually out to harm you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re trying to harm me physically or anything like that. But I do think they’re harming me by keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions about my sister. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget.” Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes or misunderstandings, but it doesn’t feel like that. I feel like information is being kept from me, and like people are trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these patterns you've noticed are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d use the word conspiracy, but it feels like it’s coordinated somehow. It’s the same pattern over and over—my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. After months of that, it doesn’t feel accidental. It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people or groups you believe are keeping information from you are actually out to harm you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re trying to harm me physically or anything like that. But I do think they’re harming me by keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions about my sister. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about people keeping information from you causing you a lot of stress or anxiety, or are they interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does cause stress. I’m still getting through my day and doing what I need to do, but it’s affecting things.\n\nFamily meetings have gotten tense, because I don’t just sit back anymore—I push for answers. And at work, I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but… people are starting to react to me differently.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"candidates": ["F20.0", "F22", "F60.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about people keeping information from you causing you a lot of stress or anxiety, or are they interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does cause stress. I’m still getting through my day and doing what I need to do, but it’s affecting things.\n\nFamily meetings have gotten tense, because I don’t just sit back anymore—I push for answers. And at work, I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but… people are starting to react to me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that people are keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions about your sister's care?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believe that.\n\nI know people keep saying these are just mistakes, but it’s been the same pattern for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people not calling me back. It doesn’t feel accidental to me anymore. It feels like they’re keeping me out and making decisions without me.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about people keeping information from you led to you having trouble keeping relationships or a job?\n\nPatient: Not trouble keeping my job, no. I’m still doing my work and handling my responsibilities.\n\nBut it has caused some strain. Family meetings are more tense now, because I push harder for answers. And at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations when I’ve pressed people about mistakes more than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but people do seem to respond to me differently now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, I think it has evolved. At first I tried to tell myself these were just separate mistakes—an appointment moved here, a bill wrong there, someone not calling me back.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, for months now, I keep seeing the same kind of pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget.” Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes or misunderstandings, but it doesn’t feel like that. I feel like information is being kept from me, and like people are trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these patterns you've noticed are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d use the word conspiracy, but it feels like it’s coordinated somehow. It’s the same pattern over and over—my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. After months of that, it doesn’t feel accidental. It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, for months now, I keep seeing the same kind of pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget.” Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes or misunderstandings, but it doesn’t feel like that. I feel like information is being kept from me, and like people are trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these patterns you've noticed are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d use the word conspiracy, but it feels like it’s coordinated somehow. It’s the same pattern over and over—my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. After months of that, it doesn’t feel accidental. It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people or groups you believe are keeping information from you are actually out to harm you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re trying to harm me physically or anything like that. But I do think they’re harming me by keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions about my sister. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about people keeping information from you causing you a lot of stress or anxiety, or are they interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does cause stress. I’m still getting through my day and doing what I need to do, but it’s affecting things.\n\nFamily meetings have gotten tense, because I don’t just sit back anymore—I push for answers. And at work, I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but… people are starting to react to me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that people are keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions about your sister's care?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believe that.\n\nI know people keep saying these are just mistakes, but it’s been the same pattern for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people not calling me back. It doesn’t feel accidental to me anymore. It feels like they’re keeping me out and making decisions without me.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about people keeping information from you led to you having trouble keeping relationships or a job?\n\nPatient: Not trouble keeping my job, no. I’m still doing my work and handling my responsibilities.\n\nBut it has caused some strain. Family meetings are more tense now, because I push harder for answers. And at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations when I’ve pressed people about mistakes more than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but people do seem to respond to me differently now.\n\nDoctor: Have your beliefs about people keeping information from you ever changed or evolved over the 11 months you've been experiencing this?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has evolved. At first I tried to tell myself these were just separate mistakes—an appointment moved here, a bill wrong there, someone not calling me back.\n\nBut over the months, it started to feel like the same pattern repeating. Now I don’t really see it as random anymore. Even when people tell me it’s just errors or miscommunication, I feel pretty sure they’re keeping information from me and trying to leave me out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a suspicious person, even before you started noticing these patterns with your sister's care?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and pay attention to details, especially with important things, but I wouldn’t say I was always suspicious of people.\n\nThis feels different to me. It’s more about what’s been happening with my sister’s care and the way people have handled things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, for months now, I keep seeing the same kind of pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget.” Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes or misunderstandings, but it doesn’t feel like that. I feel like information is being kept from me, and like people are trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these patterns you've noticed are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d use the word conspiracy, but it feels like it’s coordinated somehow. It’s the same pattern over and over—my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. After months of that, it doesn’t feel accidental. It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people or groups you believe are keeping information from you are actually out to harm you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re trying to harm me physically or anything like that. But I do think they’re harming me by keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions about my sister. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about people keeping information from you causing you a lot of stress or anxiety, or are they interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does cause stress. I’m still getting through my day and doing what I need to do, but it’s affecting things.\n\nFamily meetings have gotten tense, because I don’t just sit back anymore—I push for answers. And at work, I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but… people are starting to react to me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that people are keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions about your sister's care?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believe that.\n\nI know people keep saying these are just mistakes, but it’s been the same pattern for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people not calling me back. It doesn’t feel accidental to me anymore. It feels like they’re keeping me out and making decisions without me.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about people keeping information from you led to you having trouble keeping relationships or a job?\n\nPatient: Not trouble keeping my job, no. I’m still doing my work and handling my responsibilities.\n\nBut it has caused some strain. Family meetings are more tense now, because I push harder for answers. And at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations when I’ve pressed people about mistakes more than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but people do seem to respond to me differently now.\n\nDoctor: Have your beliefs about people keeping information from you ever changed or evolved over the 11 months you've been experiencing this?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has evolved. At first I tried to tell myself these were just separate mistakes—an appointment moved here, a bill wrong there, someone not calling me back.\n\nBut over the months, it started to feel like the same pattern repeating. Now I don’t really see it as random anymore. Even when people tell me it’s just errors or miscommunication, I feel pretty sure they’re keeping information from me and trying to leave me out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a suspicious person, even before you started noticing these patterns with your sister's care?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and pay attention to details, especially with important things, but I wouldn’t say I was always suspicious of people.\n\nThis feels different to me. It’s more about what’s been happening with my sister’s care and the way people have handled things.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trusted, like a close family member or a doctor, told you that they had investigated and found no evidence of anyone keeping information from you, would that change your beliefs?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t think it would change it completely. I’d listen, especially if it was someone I trusted, but I’ve been seeing the same pattern for months now—appointments moved, bills wrong, people not calling me back—and every time I’m told it’s just a mistake.\n\nSo if they said they found no evidence, I’d probably wonder if they just didn’t see what I’m seeing, or if they weren’t told everything. I feel pretty sure information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions about my sister.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I don’t know. I want to say yes, but honestly, after months of this same pattern, it would be hard.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, for months now, I keep seeing the same kind of pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget.” Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes or misunderstandings, but it doesn’t feel like that. I feel like information is being kept from me, and like people are trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these patterns you've noticed are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d use the word conspiracy, but it feels like it’s coordinated somehow. It’s the same pattern over and over—my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. After months of that, it doesn’t feel accidental. It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people or groups you believe are keeping information from you are actually out to harm you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re trying to harm me physically or anything like that. But I do think they’re harming me by keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions about my sister. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about people keeping information from you causing you a lot of stress or anxiety, or are they interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does cause stress. I’m still getting through my day and doing what I need to do, but it’s affecting things.\n\nFamily meetings have gotten tense, because I don’t just sit back anymore—I push for answers. And at work, I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but… people are starting to react to me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that people are keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions about your sister's care?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believe that.\n\nI know people keep saying these are just mistakes, but it’s been the same pattern for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people not calling me back. It doesn’t feel accidental to me anymore. It feels like they’re keeping me out and making decisions without me.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about people keeping information from you led to you having trouble keeping relationships or a job?\n\nPatient: Not trouble keeping my job, no. I’m still doing my work and handling my responsibilities.\n\nBut it has caused some strain. Family meetings are more tense now, because I push harder for answers. And at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations when I’ve pressed people about mistakes more than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but people do seem to respond to me differently now.\n\nDoctor: Have your beliefs about people keeping information from you ever changed or evolved over the 11 months you've been experiencing this?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has evolved. At first I tried to tell myself these were just separate mistakes—an appointment moved here, a bill wrong there, someone not calling me back.\n\nBut over the months, it started to feel like the same pattern repeating. Now I don’t really see it as random anymore. Even when people tell me it’s just errors or miscommunication, I feel pretty sure they’re keeping information from me and trying to leave me out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a suspicious person, even before you started noticing these patterns with your sister's care?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and pay attention to details, especially with important things, but I wouldn’t say I was always suspicious of people.\n\nThis feels different to me. It’s more about what’s been happening with my sister’s care and the way people have handled things.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trusted, like a close family member or a doctor, told you that they had investigated and found no evidence of anyone keeping information from you, would that change your beliefs?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t think it would change it completely. I’d listen, especially if it was someone I trusted, but I’ve been seeing the same pattern for months now—appointments moved, bills wrong, people not calling me back—and every time I’m told it’s just a mistake.\n\nSo if they said they found no evidence, I’d probably wonder if they just didn’t see what I’m seeing, or if they weren’t told everything. I feel pretty sure information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything could ever completely convince you that people are not keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I want to say yes, but honestly, after months of this same pattern, it would be hard.\n\nIf the appointments stopped getting moved without me being told, if the bills were correct, if people actually called me back and included me in decisions about my sister, maybe over time I’d feel differently. But just someone telling me “nothing is going on” wouldn’t be enough. Right now it feels too clear to me that information is being kept from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s more about what’s been happening with my sister’s care and the way people have handled things.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trusted, like a close family member or a doctor, told you that they had investigated and found no evidence of anyone keeping information from you, would that change your beliefs?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t think it would change it completely. I’d listen, especially if it was someone I trusted, but I’ve been seeing the same pattern for months now—appointments moved, bills wrong, people not calling me back—and every time I’m told it’s just a mistake.\n\nSo if they said they found no evidence, I’d probably wonder if they just didn’t see what I’m seeing, or if they weren’t told everything. I feel pretty sure information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything could ever completely convince you that people are not keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose it’s possible, but not just because someone tells me to stop believing it. I’ve been seeing this same thing for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back—and it keeps adding up.

So right now, no, I don’t think I could just stop believing it. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions about my sister. If all of that actually stopped and I was included consistently, maybe over time I could question it more.

---------- turn 19 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F22, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because, for months now, I keep seeing the same kind of pattern. My sister’s appointments get moved around, bills come in wrong, people say they’ll call me back and then they “forget.” Everyone keeps telling me it’s just mistakes or misunderstandings, but it doesn’t feel like that. I feel like information is being kept from me, and like people are trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these patterns you've noticed are part of a larger plan or conspiracy against you?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I don’t know if I’d use the word conspiracy, but it feels like it’s coordinated somehow. It’s the same pattern over and over—my sister’s appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. After months of that, it doesn’t feel accidental. It feels like they’re keeping things from me and trying to push me out of decisions I should be part of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think this pattern you've noticed is something that's being controlled by a specific person or group?\n\nPatient: I can’t point to one specific person, no. It feels more like a group of people are involved, or at least acting in the same way. The appointments getting moved, the bills being wrong, people not calling me back—it’s too consistent. I know people say it’s just mistakes, but it feels like information is being kept from me so I’m pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs you have about people keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions are based on facts that you've observed, or do you think they might be based on your interpretation of things?\n\nPatient: I think it’s based on what I’ve actually observed. I mean, I understand people can say it’s my interpretation, but when my sister’s appointments keep getting moved, and bills keep coming in wrong, and people keep “forgetting” to call me back, it doesn’t feel like random mistakes anymore. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to keep me out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these beliefs of yours could be wrong?\n\nPatient: I guess anything could be wrong, but I really don’t think this is. It’s been months of the same thing—my sister’s appointments moved, bills showing up wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back. Maybe one or two things could be mistakes, but all of it together… no. It feels clear to me that information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you think other people, besides you, have noticed this pattern of information being kept from you?\n\nPatient: I think some people have noticed parts of it, yes, but they don’t see the whole pattern the way I do. They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. So maybe they notice pieces, but I don’t think they understand what it adds up to.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these people or groups you believe are keeping information from you are actually out to harm you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they’re trying to harm me physically or anything like that. But I do think they’re harming me by keeping information from me and pushing me out of decisions about my sister. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about people keeping information from you causing you a lot of stress or anxiety, or are they interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does cause stress. I’m still getting through my day and doing what I need to do, but it’s affecting things.\n\nFamily meetings have gotten tense, because I don’t just sit back anymore—I push for answers. And at work, I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but… people are starting to react to me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that people are keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions about your sister's care?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believe that.\n\nI know people keep saying these are just mistakes, but it’s been the same pattern for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people not calling me back. It doesn’t feel accidental to me anymore. It feels like they’re keeping me out and making decisions without me.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about people keeping information from you led to you having trouble keeping relationships or a job?\n\nPatient: Not trouble keeping my job, no. I’m still doing my work and handling my responsibilities.\n\nBut it has caused some strain. Family meetings are more tense now, because I push harder for answers. And at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations when I’ve pressed people about mistakes more than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but people do seem to respond to me differently now.\n\nDoctor: Have your beliefs about people keeping information from you ever changed or evolved over the 11 months you've been experiencing this?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has evolved. At first I tried to tell myself these were just separate mistakes—an appointment moved here, a bill wrong there, someone not calling me back.\n\nBut over the months, it started to feel like the same pattern repeating. Now I don’t really see it as random anymore. Even when people tell me it’s just errors or miscommunication, I feel pretty sure they’re keeping information from me and trying to leave me out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a suspicious person, even before you started noticing these patterns with your sister's care?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and pay attention to details, especially with important things, but I wouldn’t say I was always suspicious of people.\n\nThis feels different to me. It’s more about what’s been happening with my sister’s care and the way people have handled things.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trusted, like a close family member or a doctor, told you that they had investigated and found no evidence of anyone keeping information from you, would that change your beliefs?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t think it would change it completely. I’d listen, especially if it was someone I trusted, but I’ve been seeing the same pattern for months now—appointments moved, bills wrong, people not calling me back—and every time I’m told it’s just a mistake.\n\nSo if they said they found no evidence, I’d probably wonder if they just didn’t see what I’m seeing, or if they weren’t told everything. I feel pretty sure information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything could ever completely convince you that people are not keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I want to say yes, but honestly, after months of this same pattern, it would be hard.\n\nIf the appointments stopped getting moved without me being told, if the bills were correct, if people actually called me back and included me in decisions about my sister, maybe over time I’d feel differently. But just someone telling me “nothing is going on” wouldn’t be enough. Right now it feels too clear to me that information is being kept from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop believing that people are keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s possible, but not just because someone tells me to stop believing it. I’ve been seeing this same thing for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back—and it keeps adding up.\n\nSo right now, no, I don’t think I could just stop believing it. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions about my sister. If all of that actually stopped and I was included consistently, maybe over time I could question it more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F20.0", "F22", "F60.0"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

They’ll hear that an appointment was moved or a bill was wrong and treat it like one mistake. But for me, it’s been months of the same things happening—calls not returned, information not shared, decisions being made without me. When appointments get moved, bills come in wrong, and people “forget” to call me back over and over, it feels intentional. It feels like they’re trying to make me lose control of what’s going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these beliefs about people keeping information from you for your entire life, or is this something that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, not my whole life. This started more recently—about 11 months ago, when my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I began noticing people weren’t telling me things or were leaving me out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any situation or evidence that could change your mind about people keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I mean… I’d like to think so, but honestly it would take a lot. If it were one appointment moved or one bill wrong, I could accept that as a mistake. But this has been the same pattern for months—appointments changed, wrong bills, people not calling me back, and then everyone says it’s nothing. So unless they actually started being clear with me and including me in decisions about my sister, I don’t think I’d believe it was all accidental.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned it would take a lot to change your mind about people keeping information from you, can you think of any specific evidence that would make you question your beliefs?\n\nPatient: I guess specific evidence would be if they stopped doing all these things—if my sister’s appointments weren’t suddenly moved without me knowing, if the bills were correct, and if people actually called me back and included me in decisions.\n\nBut as it is, the same pattern keeps happening. Everyone says it’s just mistakes, but after months of it, I don’t believe that. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you didn't believe people were keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: Yes. Before all this, I didn’t really think that way. It’s been going on for about 11 months now, since my sister’s health suddenly got worse and I had to start coordinating her care. That’s when I started feeling like people were keeping things from me.\n\nDoctor: Are these beliefs about people keeping information from you causing you a lot of stress or anxiety, or are they interfering with your daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does cause stress. I’m still getting through my day and doing what I need to do, but it’s affecting things.\n\nFamily meetings have gotten tense, because I don’t just sit back anymore—I push for answers. I still believe that.\n\nI know people keep saying these are just mistakes, but it’s been the same pattern for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people not calling me back. It doesn’t feel accidental to me anymore. It feels like they’re keeping me out and making decisions without me.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about people keeping information from you led to you having trouble keeping relationships or a job?\n\nPatient: Not trouble keeping my job, no. I’m still doing my work and handling my responsibilities.\n\nBut it has caused some strain. Family meetings are more tense now, because I push harder for answers. And at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations when I’ve pressed people about mistakes more than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but people do seem to respond to me differently now.\n\nDoctor: Have your beliefs about people keeping information from you ever changed or evolved over the 11 months you've been experiencing this?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has evolved. At first I tried to tell myself these were just separate mistakes—an appointment moved here, a bill wrong there, someone not calling me back.\n\nBut over the months, it started to feel like the same pattern repeating. Now I don’t really see it as random anymore. And at work, I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations because I’ve pressed people harder about routine mistakes than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but… people are starting to react to me differently.\n\nDoctor: Do you still believe that people are keeping information from you and trying to push you out of decisions about your sister's care?\n\nPatient: Yes. I still believe that.\n\nI know people keep saying these are just mistakes, but it’s been the same pattern for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people not calling me back. It doesn’t feel accidental to me anymore. It feels like they’re keeping me out and making decisions without me.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs about people keeping information from you led to you having trouble keeping relationships or a job?\n\nPatient: Not trouble keeping my job, no. I’m still doing my work and handling my responsibilities.\n\nBut it has caused some strain. Family meetings are more tense now, because I push harder for answers. And at work I’ve had a few uncomfortable conversations when I’ve pressed people about mistakes more than I probably used to. So I’m managing, but people do seem to respond to me differently now.\n\nDoctor: Have your beliefs about people keeping information from you ever changed or evolved over the 11 months you've been experiencing this?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it has evolved. At first I tried to tell myself these were just separate mistakes—an appointment moved here, a bill wrong there, someone not calling me back.\n\nBut over the months, it started to feel like the same pattern repeating. Now I don’t really see it as random anymore. Even when people tell me it’s just errors or miscommunication, I feel pretty sure they’re keeping information from me and trying to leave me out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Have you always been a suspicious person, even before you started noticing these patterns with your sister's care?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve always tried to be careful and pay attention to details, especially with important things, but I wouldn’t say I was always suspicious of people.\n\nThis feels different to me. It’s more about what’s been happening with my sister’s care and the way people have handled things.\n\nDoctor: If someone you trusted, like a close family member or a doctor, told you that they had investigated and found no evidence of anyone keeping information from you, would that change your beliefs?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don’t think it would change it completely. I’d listen, especially if it was someone I trusted, but I’ve been seeing the same pattern for months now—appointments moved, bills wrong, people not calling me back—and every time I’m told it’s just a mistake.\n\nSo if they said they found no evidence, I’d probably wonder if they just didn’t see what I’m seeing, or if they weren’t told everything. I feel pretty sure information is being kept from me and I’m being pushed out of decisions about my sister.\n\nDoctor: Do you think anything could ever completely convince you that people are not keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I want to say yes, but honestly, after months of this same pattern, it would be hard.\n\nIf the appointments stopped getting moved without me being told, if the bills were correct, if people actually called me back and included me in decisions about my sister, maybe over time I’d feel differently. But just someone telling me “nothing is going on” wouldn’t be enough. Right now it feels too clear to me that information is being kept from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you could ever stop believing that people are keeping information from you?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s possible, but not just because someone tells me to stop believing it. I’ve been seeing this same thing for months—appointments getting moved, bills being wrong, people saying they forgot to call me back—and it keeps adding up.\n\nSo right now, no, I don’t think I could just stop believing it. It feels like they’re keeping information from me and trying to push me out of decisions about my sister. If all of that actually stopped and I was included consistently, maybe over time I could question it more.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that others cannot?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t heard voices or seen things that other people couldn’t see. At least, not that I’ve noticed."
